Evidence packets contain verbatim excerpts, not full sources. Omitted passages may contain qualifications or counterevidence. Do not infer absence from omission; acknowledge this limitation. Cite text exactly or use the original segment_id and timestamps.

Tenderness in one small spot on the bone, pain at rest, or pain at night is a red flag for a stress fracture and needs a clinician.\n\nSudden, severe lower-leg pain needs emergency care. Don't treat it as ordinary shin splints or wait for it to settle after another session.\n\nA 2025 prevention review supported neuromuscular training and some insoles, but it does not validate a particular strength-and-balance routine for ruckers.\n\nRecovery can take weeks to months. That explains why the pack matters. The study measured forces, not the rate of MTSS in recreational ruckers.\n\nMTSS and tibial bone stress injuries can cause overlapping symptoms and probably sit on the same continuum. One clinical reference describes MTSS tenderness as running along more than about 5 cm (2 inches) of the inner shin edge, while point tenderness over a shorter stretch of bone points toward a stress fracture (Larson et al., StatPearls, 2025). Tenderness in one spot, pain at rest or at night, swelling, or pain with ordinary walking should prompt medical evaluation rather than a home diagnosis of either condition.\n\nPrimary causes in loaded walking\n\nMTSS is multifactorial. A 2017 meta-analysis of risk factors in active people found five with consistent evidence: female sex, higher body weight, greater navicular drop (the arch flattening more under load), a previous running injury, and greater hip external rotation measured with the hip flexed (Reinking et al., 2017). These are associations, not a diagnosis or a reason to try to change you"},{"text":" systematic review included 12 randomized prevention trials with 8,197 participants; 10 trials were eligible for meta-analysis. It reported high-certainty support for neuromuscular training and moderate-certainty support for overpronation insoles. Shock-absorbing insoles had no statistically significant preventive effect, and static stretching and special socks lacked supporting evidence. These findings do not establish a rucking-specific prevention program.\n\nManage training load. Change one variable at a time, then judge symptoms and recovery before changing another. There is no evidence-based weekly percentage that guarantees prevention.\n\nUse terrain you can control. Changing surfaces can vary the demand, but surface rotation is not a proven MTSS-prevention protocol. Uneven or sloped ground may increase symptoms for some people.\n\nChoose comfortable footwear. Transition gradually when heel-to-toe drop, cushioning, stiffness, or fit changes substantially. A max-cushion option such as the Hoka Bondi 9 may feel comfortable on pavement, but cushioning does not guarantee MTSS prevention.\n\nTrack distance, pack weight, pace, terrain, and symptoms. AAOS advises at least two pain-free weeks before returning to exercise, then less frequent, shorter, easier sessions. That is a recovery criterion, not a promise of recovery two weeks after pain starts or clearance for a suspected stress fracture.\n\nWhen to seek medical attention\n\nArrange medical evaluation for pain that is focal, worsening, present at rest or at night, or not settling after you stop the aggravating training. Those features can occur with conditions other than MTSS and should not be diagnosed from an article.\n\nGet urgent same-day advice for trouble bearing weight, marked swelling, worsening pain, or new numbness or weakness. Don't try another ruck while waiting for assessment.\n\nSeek emergency care for sudden, severe lower-leg pain, which can occur with acute compartment syndrome. An injured leg or foot that is numb, tingling, cold, or discolored also needs emergency assessment. You don't need to have all these signs.\n\nRedness and warmth with fever or rapidly increasing swelling needs urgent care. New one-sided"},{"text":" calf pain and swelling can also be a sign of DVT and needs urgent assessment; accompanying breathlessness or chest pain is an emergency.\n\nRecovery and return-to-training protocols\n\nRecovery depends on the diagnosis, symptom severity, and training demand. Complete rest, continued pain-free activity, and return timing should be chosen for the individual rather than prescribed by a fixed calendar.\n\nStep 1: Settle the aggravating load. Avoid activities that reproduce or increase pain. Gentle movement or cross-training can continue only when comfortable. Static stretching is not a proven MTSS treatment.\n\nStep 2: Recheck ordinary walking. Once daily activity is pain-free, use a short unloaded walk on predictable terrain. Check symptoms during the walk, later that day, and the next morning. This is a progress check, not clearance for a suspected bone stress injury.\n\nStep 3: Reintroduce loaded walking. Follow the pain-free recovery period described above or your clinician's plan. Begin with both a lighter pack and a shorter route than before the injury. Keep pace and terrain easy to control, and stop if shin pain returns.\n\nStep 4: Build toward the required training. Cold may offer short-term comfort after symptoms begin, but it does not diagnose or heal the cause. Protect the skin, stop if it hurts, and do not use numbness to continue a painful ruck.\n\nThere is no evidence-based percentage. Sudden one-sided numbness or weakness, facial droop, or speech trouble needs emergency help, even if it clears.\n\nWhat's happening\n\nRucksack palsy is a load-carriage-related nerve injury involving compression or traction of the brachial plexus, with shoulder straps as the usual mechanism. Symptoms can include tingling, numbness, pain, cramping, or weakness in the upper limb (Knapik et al., 2016). Similar symptoms can also come from the neck, a single peripheral nerve, circulation, or another condition, so numb hands under a pack are not a diagnosis by themselves.\n\nMild ting"},{"text":"ling that settles completely after the pack comes off may be transient pressure. That doesn't establish the cause or make continuing safe. Diagnosed rucksack palsy can take months to recover, and some patients have lasting symptoms (Nylund et al., 2011). Stop, remove the pack, and end the loaded session; don't wait for weakness or persistent numbness to wear off under load.\n\nThat evidence supports a simple boundary: treat any new tingling as a signal to remove the pressure, not as proof that no damage has occurred. Do not continue rucking through weakness or persistent numbness while waiting for it to \"loosen up.\"\n\nThe brachial plexus runs from the neck through the shoulder into the arm. A load-carriage review names heavier load, longer carrying distance, and poor load distribution as hypothetical risk factors, with strap compression or traction as the mechanism. It frames its prevention measures as ways symptoms may be reduced rather than as tested programs (Knapik et al., 2016).\n\n01Knapik and colleagues describe rucksack palsy as a compression or traction injury and recommend reducing load, improving distribution, adjusting and padding straps, and considering a hip belt.\n\n02Nylun"},{"text":"d and colleagues found that 30 of 38 military patients (79%) reported no symptoms at long-term follow-up; eight (21%) still reported symptoms. Symptom reports and measured muscle strength were separate outcomes.\n\n03Dorhout Mees and colleagues reviewed 63 Dutch military cases, including 52 with sensory symptoms (83%). Their recovery table lists four complete, 44 partial and one absent recovery: 49 recorded outcomes, with a median follow-up of nine weeks. Those short, incomplete records cannot establish a permanent-disability rate.\n\n04The exact rules in this guide, such as one finger of strap slack or a timed fit check, are practical field cues rather than clinically validated thresholds.\n\nFive fit factors worth checking\n\nThe following factors can increase shoulder pressure or nerve traction. They are useful fit checks, not a way to diagnose the source of numbness.\n\nHigh shoulder-strap pressure. Over-tightened straps can concentrate pressure across the shoulder. Straps should control the pack without creating a hard pressure line, but there is no universal \"correct\" notch for every body and pack.\n\nNarrow or poorly padded straps. Narrow straps can concentrate a given load over less su"},{"text":"rface area. A load-carriage review recommends wider, better-padded straps as one way to reduce traction and compression symptoms (Knapik et al., 2016).\n\nMore load than you or the pack can distribute comfortably. In the Finnish series, heavier recalled loads at symptom onset were associated with reduced muscle strength at follow-up; they did not significantly predict self-reported symptoms (Nylund et al., 2011). That association doesn't establish a safe weight. New symptoms mean the pack comes off.\n\nNo effective load transfer. A properly fitted hip belt can shift some load away from the shoulders. The Knapik review recommends considering one, but neither a 15-pound threshold nor a guaranteed percentage of transfer applies to every pack and torso.\n\nHolding the straps for long periods. Gripping the straps can keep the arms and shoulders in one position and can mask a poor fit. Let the arms swing naturally when terrain allows. This is a practical comfort cue; the cited studies do not establish strap-gripping as a measured cause of rucksack palsy.\n\nWhat to do if your hands go numb\n\nIf tingling or numbness begins under the pack, stop rather than trying to diagnose it while walking. One finger of strap slack is a useful field cue, not a medical threshold.\n\nThere is no cited five-minute cutoff that separates harmless pressure from nerve injury. Join now to get the newsletter from its first issue. Unsubscribe anytime.\n\nMuch load-carriage research comes from soldiers carrying heavier loads, farther, with less recovery than a recreational ruck, so it doesn't map directly onto a neighborhood session.\n\nA pack changes trunk demands, but evidence that recreational rucking treats back pain or improves back-muscle endurance is limited.\n\nBack pain can have many causes. Excess load, poor fit, fatigue, and rapid progression are possible contributors.\n\nGet clinical guidance before rucking with a disc injury, spinal stenosis, prior surgery, neurological symptoms, or persistent back pain.\n\nBack pain with new bladder or bowel changes, saddle numbness, or symptoms in both legs needs emergency assessment. Don't wait for symptoms to become severe.\n\nThe short answer\n\nRecreational rucking adds load and changes how your trunk works. A short neighborhood walk with a light pack is a different exposure from a heavy military march, but neither the label \"recr"},{"text":"eational\" nor a particular weight makes it safe for everyone. Rucking isn't an established treatment for back pain.\n\nBack pain with new difficulty passing urine, loss of bladder or bowel control, numbness around the genitals or anus (the saddle area), or pain, tingling, numbness or weakness in both legs needs emergency assessment now. New loss of genital sensation or sexual function is another warning sign. These can indicate serious nerve compression, including cauda equina syndrome; don't wait for severe pain or complete incontinence (NHS).\n\nWhat the military research shows\n\nMuch of the load-carriage injury research comes from military populations. Its load, distance, pace, equipment, and limited recovery must be considered before applying it to recreational rucking.\n\nReviews of soldier load carriage list back strains and back problems among the common injuries, alongside foot blisters, stress fractures, knee pain and rucksack palsy (Knapik et al., 2004; Knapik, 2014). Heavier loads raise energy cost, change gait and speed up fatigue, all of which can raise injury risk. Soldiers often carry those loads for long distances, under time pressure, with limited recovery.\n\nA systematic "},{"text":"review of load-carriage systems found that carrying loads may provoke low back pain and that pack weight can influence perceived pain, but the 47 studies were too varied to pool and their findings conflicted (Golriz and Walker, 2011).\n\nFor any load carriage study, look at the actual weight, pace, distance, terrain and recovery. A recreational event can still be a demanding exposure; a military label alone doesn't tell you the dose either.\n\nBanks et al., 2026 studied 12 healthy adults carrying dumbbells at 7.5% and 15% of body weight in one or two hands, using a walking boot to induce gait asymmetry. Gait asymmetry mostly didn't change normalized peak low-back forces; the exception was the heavier one-handed carry opposite the boot. Modeled compression and shear were below published manual-handling tissue tolerances. That comparison doesn't establish an injury-free dose: the study didn't test backpacks, long-term injury risk or people with back pain. Our research digest covers the study separately.\n\nWhat recreational rucking does for your back\n\nLoaded walking changes posture and trunk demands. Those immediate responses aren't proof of stronger back muscles, pain relief or lasting po"},{"text":"l adjustment to carrying a pack. If the load forces an uncomfortable position or changes how you walk, reduce it or stop. Neither a forward bend nor a perfectly upright stance diagnoses disc stress or predicts injury on its own.\n\nFatigue outpacing the session. Distance, hills, speed and weight all contribute to demand. Back soreness alone doesn't show that your trunk muscles are weak; consider the whole session and your recovery.\n\nPre-existing disc issues not being managed. If you have a known herniated disc, stenosis, or previous serious back injury, rucking needs to be progressive and often requires specific modifications or professional guidance.\n\nSudden load jumps. No weekly increase is automatically safe. Bone-health evidence from weighted-vest trials is mixed, and no trial has tested a rucksack.\n\nResearch finds menstrual-cycle effects on performance trivial on average, with no basis for a universal phase-based rule. Treat it as a test load; there's no need to outgrow it quickly.\n\nUse the lower end if you are returning from inactivity, have a smaller frame, are postpartum, or have a history of foot, knee, hip, back, or pelvic-floor symptoms. A person with years of strength and hiking experience may be comfortable beginning above that range, but the first session should still feel controlled.\n\nThe useful checks are visible and repeatable:\n\nYour normal walking gait does not change.\n\nYou can stand tall without leaning forward.\n\nThe pack does not bounce, pull away from your back, or create numbness.\n\nYou finish feeling as though you could have continued another 10 minutes.\n\nOrdinary muscle fatigue settles; joint pain, tingling, numbness, or increasing back pain is a stop signal, and symptoms that persist after you stop deserve a clinician's look.\n\nA classic review of load carriage found that energy cost, forward trunk lean and ground forces all rise as pack weight goes up ("},{"text":"Knapik et al., 1996). Neither study supports one load percentage that is right for every woman.\n\nTreat the calculator result as a planning ceiling. It isn't an instruction to carry that much on day one.\n\nFit the pack to your body\n\nMeasure your torso from the prominent C7 vertebra at the base of your neck to the line between the tops of your hip bones. Then try the pack with weight inside it. Height and gender labels are only shortcuts; the loaded fit is what counts.\n\nCheck these five points:\n\nHarness range: The pack's stated torso range includes your measurement.\n\nShoulder spacing: The straps wrap without cutting into the neck or sitting at the edge of the shoulders.\n\nSternum strap: It can sit comfortably across the upper chest without pressure on breast tissue.\n\nLoad position: Dense weight stays high, secure, and close to the back instead of sagging away from it.\n\nHip belt, when used: It lands on the hip crest and transfers load without digging into the abdomen.\n\nWo"},{"text":"men-specific harnesses can help when their strap shape or torso range matches you, but a smaller unisex ruck may fit better than a poorly matched women's model. That can make rucking a useful part of a fat-loss plan, particularly if it is more enjoyable and repeatable than running. It does not create a women-specific hormonal fat-loss effect. Long-term weight change still depends on total activity, food intake, recovery, and consistency.\n\nThe bone-health case deserves similar restraint. Weight-bearing and resistance exercise are important for bone and muscle health, but no trial has tested rucking for bone, and the weighted-vest trials point in different directions:\n\nA 2026 meta-analysis of nine trials in middle-aged and older adults found that vest-plus-exercise programs raised hip and spine bone density compared with usual activity, but adding a vest to exercise people alre"},{"text":"ady did showed no confirmed extra bone benefit, and the bone evidence was rated low to moderate certainty (Wang et al., 2026).\n\nIn the OsteoACTIVE trial, 80 postmenopausal women with low bone density and a healed wrist fracture did six months of weight-vest exercise plus education. A year on, their strength, balance and bone density were no different from a group that got education alone (Hakestad et al., 2015).\n\nIn a 2025 randomized trial of 150 older adults, three-quarters of them women, wearing a weighted vest did not prevent hip bone loss during intentional weight loss (Beavers et al., 2025).\n\nThat makes rucking a reasonable form of weight-bearing exercise. Smartwool sells its merino base layers on odor resistance and temperature regulation; this page looks at which of its fabric weights and constructions make sense for rucking.\n\nThis is a specification-led review of where Smartwool's fabric weights, blends, fit, and care requirements make sense for loaded walking. Body-mapped knit zones belong to the separate Intraknit range.\n\nFit Options: Slim fit, which Smartwool says follows the contours of the body, in men's and women's cuts. The men's crews run S to XXL, and the Classic Thermal also comes in XS.\n\nCare: Follow the label for the exact garment. Smartwool's general wool-care guidance also recommends mild detergent, no fabric softener, and air drying when possible.\n\nSmartwool says the All-Season's recycled nylon core adds durability. It is the most logical starting point for mild-weather rucking because the lighter fabric is intended for year-round activity and layering.\n\nClassic Thermal: The current crew is a 250 g/m², 100% merino layer intended for cool-to-cold conditions. Ruck Authority has not completed an abrasion or wash-cycle field test, so this page does not assign a lifespan to any model.\n\nSmartwool attributes moisture management, odor resistance, and temperature regulation to merino wool. Fabric preference still requires an individual fit check.\n\nNot Ideal For: Budget-conscious ruckers doing mostly day training, or anyone who wants the most abrasion-resistant fabric, which usually means a synthetic. For hiking or other high-output movement, wear a lightweight base layer underneath to avoid overheating. Around camp or in cold weather, add a fleece or light midlayer for extra warmth. Because down vests aren’t waterproof, pair them with a shell or rain jacket in wet conditions—or wear them under a ski jacket for added insulation on cold winter days.\n\nShould a down vest fit tight or loose?\n\nA down vest should fit snug but not tight. It needs enough room for the down to fully loft and trap warm air, but not so much space that cold air seeps in. If you plan to layer underneath, choose a slightly roomier fit; for wearing over a base layer, a trimmer cut keeps warmth close to your core.\n\nWhat's the difference between down and synthetic insulation?\n\nDown insulation (made from goose or duck feathers) is lighter, warmer for its weight, and more compressible—perfect for packability and dry, cold conditions. Its main drawbacks are cost, delicacy, and loss of warmth when wet.\n\nSynthetic insulation is typically heavier and bulkier but stays warm when damp, dries faster, and is easier to care for. It should trap warmth close to your core without compressing the down, which reduces loft and insulation. Leave enough room to fit a sweater or light jacket underneath, and make sure the armholes aren’t restrictive, so you can move freely and layer as needed.\n\nWhat's the difference between down and synthetic insulation?\n\nDown insulation comes from the fine plumage of ducks or geese. It’s extremely light, warm, and compressible, making it ideal for dry, cold conditions. However, it loses warmth when wet and requires more care.\n\nSynthetic insulation, made from polyester fibers, mimics down’s loft but retains heat even when damp and dries faster. It’s easier to wash and usually more affordable, but slightly heavier and less packable than down.\n\nAre insulated vests warm enough for extreme cold?\n\nNot by themselves. Insulated vests keep your core warm, but since they don’t cover your arms, they’re best used as a layering piece in extreme cold. It either requires huffing and puffing or capturing air in a stuff sack and pumping it, which is significantly less fun to do if it is windy, buggy, cold, rainy, or you're at altitude. With 3.5\" and 4\" inflatable backpacking pads becoming the trend, there is more inflating needed than ever. Pad pumps, aka sleeping pad inflators, use batteries and fans to inflate your sleeping pad, eliminating one of the worst camp chores.\n\nElectric pad inflators are USB-powered or battery-powered, and are faster and require less human power and attention than manual pumps, pump sacks that come with your pad, or breathing into your pad. Pad pumps are often lighter than manual pump sacks, too. This guide doesn't cover manual sleeping pad inflators, which I find annoying and way less convenient than electric pad inflators.\n\nWe spent 1.5 years testing as many sleeping pad inflators as we could find, from "},{"text":"those that work for every backpacking pad or camping mattress out there to ones that only work for one manufacturer. We tested while backpacking on the Arizona Trail and off-trail on the Grand Enchantment Trail, as well as while backpacking in Northern California, the Washington Coast, the Eastern Sierra, Colorado, and while thru-hiking the West Coast Trail. We also ran side-by-side lab tests, noting inflation time on the most popular standard test pads, battery drain, valve compatibility, and noise measured by decibels.\n\nTo judge pad pumps, we considered weight, inflation speed, battery life, power sources, valve compatibility, noise level, durability, and value. We covered everything from sub-30g USB fans to 100g+ multi-function electric vacuum pumps. Ultimately, the right pick depends heavily on what pad you have and how much you value weight vs. speed.\n\nTop pick: The Alpenglow Gear Alpenblow Lux is the best overall backpacking sleeping pad inflator. With an inflator, deflator, easy USB-C operation off your phone, and features like a noise machine and camping lantern, it has quickly become a must-have for ultralight backpackers looking to reduce their camp chores and is so cleve"},{"text":"enjoy doing.\n\nWe also appreciate the adaptability of the Alpenblow Lux, which works with nearly every brand of backpacking pad. The Alpenglow Lux comes with four sleeping pad valve adapters, which are clearly marked with diagrams on its packing tube. You can buy adapters for three other brands of sleeping pads. It comes with four valve adapters that work for Therm-a-Rest, NEMO, Sea to Summit, Big Agnes, and EXPED pads.\n\nWhile not the fastest or lightest pump out there, the Alpenblow Lux hit all the marks we looked for in a pad inflator and then some without costing more than competitors. The Lux is the best overall pad inflator for backpackers, ultralighters, and anyone who hates inflating their backpacking pad.\n\nThe Alpenblow Lux took us about 2 minutes to fully inflate a NEMO Tensor All-Season sleeping pad. It registered only 40 dB on our noise-measuring app, which is better than we can say for other pad pumps. The original Alpenglow is also noticeably quieter than the Lux and wins the aw"},{"text":"ors\n\nUSB-powered vs. self-contained electric vs. manual\n\nSleeping pad inflators work several ways: battery-powered or manual. We didn't consider any manual pumps or pumpsacks in this guide (but if you are wondering, the best pumpsack out there for a backpacking pad is the one made by Sea to Summit. Alas, it only works with Sea to Summit pads.)\n\nUSB-powered inflators work by connecting your phone or power bank via a USB cord to the inflator.\n\nSelf-contained electric inflators have a small lithium battery inside the inflator. You charge the whole unit or just the battery separately. No power bank or phone battery drainage required.\n\nWeight and system weight\n\nFor a backpacking pad inflator, weight is likely one of your most important criteria. Consider the weight of the unit and its adapters (and included cords and batteries). If you're on a shorter backpacking trip, you may not be carrying a power bank. So if you're just carrying a power bank for your pad inflator, that is a lot of extra weight to add to the equation.\n\nFor a car camping mattress inflator, weight will matter less. In fact, we recommend the heavier-duty inflators for car camping. Inflating camping mattresses manually i"},{"text":"efore going to sleep. But I hate it. Plus, backpacking pads are getting thicker these days, with many top pads at nearly 4\" tall! The pad pump changes everything and makes one of the worst camp chores into an easy part of an automated pre-sleep routine.\n\nFor car camping mattresses, I think a pad inflator is essential. If you're spending camping mattress dollars for a good night of sleep, I think the extra ~$60 to get a pad inflator is more than worth it. Camping has enough chores as it is. Spending 30 minutes taking turns manually inflating a camping mattress is the sort of camp chore that teaches kids that camping is not fun and makes adults never want to camp again. Get an easy-to-use, easy-to-connect mattress pump, and it is a total camping game changer.\n\nValve compatibility guide\n\nUnfortunately, there is no universal pad valve. Some of the other sleeping bags we tested side"},{"text":" by side that claim to be 15°F bags felt cold under similar conditions, even though we were testing them literally side by side in the same tent and on the same NEMO Tensor Extreme Conditions sleeping pad.\n\nIt's worth noting that Exped did their temperature rating assuming you are using a sleeping pad with an R-value of 7. R-value is a measure of insulative capability. The R-value of your sleeping pad can arguably be more important than your sleeping bag's temperature rating for keeping you warm. Most independent tests for sleeping bag warmth assume you're sleeping on a pad with an R-value of 5, whereas we occasionally see an R-value of 7+ be rated for -40°F temperatures. That means we take the temperature ratings listed in the name with a grain of salt.\n\nExped recommends using a sleeping pad with an R-value of at least 6.5. You can read more about R-value in our sleeping pads guide.\n\nComfort and fit\n\nThe mummy shape on the Exped Terra 15 sleeping bag was quite comfortable. We tested in a long wide size and found it reminiscent of the Spoon shape on the NEMO Disco sleeping bag. There was room to move; the hood was easy to secure. Insulation varies by material and thickness. How much insulation you need directly correlates with the temperatures you plan to explore. The colder the climate, the better (and thicker) the insulation should be. Check out each of our picks to find out why we like the insulation for each boot. For sub-zero temperatures, thicker insulation, such as wool or high-loft synthetic fills, is best. If you only need moderate warmth, look for lighter insulation.\n\nHow to Choose the Winter Boot for You\n\nSide-by-side comparison of just a handful of the winter boots we tested.\n\nDo you live somewhere with extremely cold weather?\n\nThere are good options for extremely cold weather, depending on what style you’re lookin"},{"text":"ng more time in deep snow, sustained cold, or icy conditions, a dedicated winter boot usually gives you more insulation, a taller shaft, and winter-specific traction.\n\nFor actual winter hiking, we prefer boots with good ankle support, secure lacing, and enough tread for snow and ice.\n\nWhat socks should I wear with winter boots?\n\nWe like wool socks for winter because they add warmth without holding onto moisture the way cotton does. The right thickness depends on the boot and how much room you have inside it.\n\nWhen trying on winter boots, test them with more than one sock thickness. Your toes should still have some wiggle room, and your heel shouldn’t slide around. On warmer days, a thinner sock may be enough. When it gets colder, having room for a thicker wool sock gives you more flexibility.\n\nWhat are the warmest types of winter boots?\n\nThe warmest boots in this guide generally combine substantial insulation with a thick sole and enough height to keep snow out. Ad supported."},"selection_reason":"Relevance, Credibility, and Freshness and novelty support acceptance for Pieces 01, 02, and 03: newly reported testing includes a 2 lb. 5.5 oz. measured weight, a battery-out freezer soak, rain and fog ranging limitations, comparative optics and ballistic checks, and a shifting diopter, all useful for assessing Mammoth tradeoffs. Under Commercial incentives and Uncertainty, Rokslide is ad supported and includes a purchase link that must be omitted; product provision or payment is unspecified, findings remain attributed to this reviewer, and long-term durability and the three-independent-publisher basis for an article's central argument remain unestablished.","excerpted":true,"passages":[{"text":"More and more electro-optics are coming to market for the western hunter. Multiple use is the name of the game, and a single device that replaces two or more makes all the more sense to hunters that are already stuffing their pack with gear.\n\nThis is why range-finding binoculars make so much sense. They eliminate the need for a separate binocular and laser rangefinder, and add the convenience of having ranges available immediately. Add in onboard environmental sensors, inclinometers, compasses, and advanced ballistic solvers, and you are really eliminating some gear.\n\nSounds like a no-brainer, right? The issue has always been that there are required compromises when bringing optics, lasers, and displays together. Early versions were noticeably poor optically. So much so, many hunters returned to separate binoculars and rangefinders.\n\nFlagship RF binos from the big European companies of Zeiss, Leica, and Swarovski largely mitigated the issue with their recent models, and now hunters have the luxury of having top-shelf optics and ballistic rangefinding in one package. The rub on those? Well, that’s the usual one: they cost as much as that high-mileage used car you’ve been looking at "},{"text":"for your teen driver.\n\nOther companies have brought to market more budget-friendly options. Generally, these mid-tier offerings have excellent rangefinding and advanced ballistics–but pair that up with optical performance that, while improved from the 1st generation, still falls behind better mid-priced dedicated binoculars in optical performance.\n\nThe Jons (Jon Allen and Jon LaCorte) of Tract Optics intended to shake up that status quo with the introduction of the Tract Toric LRF 10×42 Rangefinding binocular by improving optical performance and combining it with an advanced laser and computations from Eagle Ballistics.\n\nAt just under 6 inches long, the Toric LRF is more than a half inch shorter than the Zeiss Victory RF’s 7.6 inches. The Toric LRF is compact, but not exactly lightweight. The Toric LRF tips the scale at 2 lbs. 5.5 ounces with an Outdoorsman’s stud attached. The physically larger Victory RF (also with an Outdoorsman’s stud) is actually lighter at 2 lbs. 3 ounces.\n\nThe Toric LRF is fully rubber armored with non-removable 4-position twist-out eyecups. Push-pull “locking” diopter rings are on each barrel. The left barrel adjusts the optic, and the right adjusts the dis"},{"text":"play. The locking feature is rather loose, and I did have one occasion where the diopter had shifted out and moved slightly, so that is something for the hunter to keep an eye on. Additionally, adjusting the screen will affect the optic diopter, so make sure to adjust the screen first, and then move to the left barrel. One note on this. I normally have diopters close to the “0” setting on most optics. In order to get the screen sharp, I had to adjust the screen diopter pretty far to the “-“ side. Accordingly, I had to adjust the left diopter far “-“ as well, and ended up near the far limit of adjustment. Still, after adjustment, I ended up with a sharp, easy observation view and crisp display.\n\nThe Toric LRF is powered by a single CR2 battery that is housed within the focus wheel.\n\nThis conveniently allows the front hinge to accommodate standard 1/4-20 adapters. I immediately mounted a Outdoorsman’s binoculars adapter stud and left it on indefinitely..\n\nThe right side of the Toric LRF has a single rubberized button marked “FIRE.” The button is raised slightly from the surrounding armor and has stippling around the text. The left side has four buttons. Opposite the FIRE button is a "},{"text":"it had not moved. I did a 10-hour cold soak in the freezer with the battery removed. I pulled the frosty Toric LRF out of the freezer, put in the battery, uncapped the lenses, and immediately received instant ranges with ballistic information. Of course, like any frozen optic, it did not take long for the exterior lenses to ice over in the warmer air, naturally interfering with observation and ranging, but I could see the display operating within the right barrel. As the Toric LRF equalized with the air temperature, everything returned to proper working order.\n\nThe Toric LRF has a suitably wide FOV at 330 ft. at 1000 yards. This is similar to traditional “wide field” 10×42 roof prism binoculars, but falls just short of the European competition like the Zeiss at 345 ft. at 1000 yards.\n\nApparent sharpness is excellent, and I could resolve the same element on the chart as I could with the Zeiss Victory RF in close-range chart testing. This wasn’t too surprising as both optics have a relatively low 10x magnification, but I have seen lower apparent resolution on some other mid-tier RF models from other manufacturers, so this was a good sign for the Toric LRF.\n\nColor bias and transmissio"},{"text":"n are where the expensive European models often have a clear advantage over the mid- and lower-tier options. In normal use, the Toric LRF has a pretty neutral to slightly cool image and doesn’t have the obvious color issues of other mid-tier competitors. However, when looking at a white background, I did notice a warm bias at the top of the image and a cool bias at the bottom. This led to testing the left and right barrels individually by shining light from the objective ends and observing the pupil left on a white sheet of paper. In this testing, it was clear the one barrel had a warm bias, and the other barrel was cool. This difference was much lower than other RF binoculars I have looked at, but still present.\n\nRunning the same test on the Zeiss yielded no apparent difference between barrels.\n\nTo be absolutely clear, this result for the Toric LRF is not poor. The color difference is small, and color issues during glassing are not noticeable under most conditions. In fact, the Tract has the mildest version of this effect I have seen from a non-Euro RF binocular.\n\nLow light performance is quite good. The Tract did a good job keeping up with the much more expensive Zeiss until the "},{"text":"very end of twilight, when the Zeiss did appear a touch brighter. Still, the Toric LRF had very good performance for an RF binocular.\n\nDistortion is very well controlled in the Toric LRF. There is a low amount of pincushion distortion, which is almost identical to that of the Victory RF\n\nAt the edge, fringing is easily visible and more intense than the Zeiss.\n\nThe Tract Toric LRF has the best optics I have tested in a non-Euro RF binocular. I would gladly use the Toric LRF as my primary optic, and it pushes the very best competition in performance, even if it doesn’t quite match them.\n\nThe rangefinding capabilities of the Toric LRF are what really stand out with this device. The on-board sensors include temperature, pressure, inclination, and compass bearing. The device syncs via Bluetooth with the Eagle Ballistics app. The device can store five profiles at a time. The ballistic display can be customized to display environmentals, earthbase effects (sic), bullet flight, windage and elevation, gun profile, and startup logo. The hunter can switch on and off any of those options.\n\nThe Toric LRF reticle is customizable with circle, cross, oval, 1 MOA dot, and 2 MOA dot options.\n\nOne of"},{"text":" level. This is an excellent tool for hunters. The optics are very, very good, and just behind the Euro leaders. The rangefinder is as good as anything available. The Toric LRF’s ballistic rangefinding capabilities are more extensive and precise than even those from Zeiss and Swarovski’s top RF offerings, while being highly competitive with the brand-new Leica Geovid MAX, which costs DOUBLE the Toric LRF.\n\nEagle Ballistics is a full-featured app and on-device ballistics engine that integrates with the Toric LRF effortlessly.\n\nConsidering the price and performance, the Toric LRF is easily one of the most impressive optics I have seen in recent years.\n\nI need more time with the Toric LRF to really evaluate the long-term durability, but it is comforting to know Tract covers the Toric LRF with the “Tract Trust Assurance” warranty, which is their fully transferable lifetime warranty that covers both the optics AND electronics. Not many electro-optics are covered so thoroughly. It serves as pantry, dresser, optics case, first aid cabinet, seat, and so many other things. The best hunting backpacks do all of those things, but also haul that precious cargo of meat out after the tags are punched.\n\nWay back in 2011, I used the original KUIU Icon frame and bag and appreciated the quality craftsmanship and clever organization. The carbon frame was innovative and did a good job with moderate loads. Under really heavy loads, though, it got overwhelmed, and some hunters had durability issues, including frames cracking and breaking. KUIU went through more iterations and improvements, always preserving their organizational prowess but improving fit and finish. Performance did improve, and many hunters really found the newer Icon Pro and Pro LT to be a good fit for their hunts. I liked the weight and function of the newer packs, but still felt heavy loads got too uncomfortable, and the suspension just didn’t quite fit me right.\n\nSignificantly im"},{"text":"prove heavy-weight carrying capacity and comfort.\n\nSimplify the overall design while dramatically improving performance.\n\nTo reach these goals, KUIU started from scratch, which means the new Icon 6 packs and frames are not backward compatible with the older Icons, including the Pro and Pro LT models.\n\nIt all starts with the new patent-pending frame, which combines a “corrugated carbon architecture” made of thermoplastic carbon fiber. The new frame is taller and wider than previous KUIU frames and is specifically designed to flex where it is needed and stay stiff for load-bearing. The new frame promises increased strength and durability as well as improved “damage tolerance.” KUIU is confident the new frame satisfies design goal #1.\n\nThe suspension fits through hook-and-loop attachment and offers excellent adjustability in both length and angle. The hip belt and shoulder straps have been redesigned in shape and materials, with new foam mapped to be thinner and thicker depending on anatomic need. A thick lumbar pad matches a new wider hip belt.\n\nThe available pack bags include the tested 3500 CI daypack, a versatile 4500 CI option, a multi-day 6000 CI freighter, and an expedition-rea"},{"text":"dy 8200 CI behemoth. Quality 330 denier Cordura and Waterproof Cordura are the primary fabrics used in the construction of the packs. My 3500 test pack is well made with quality stitching and materials throughout. Packs attach to the frame simply by placing the packbag’s hypalon stay pockets over the top stays and tensioning by way of two hook-and-loop straps under the frame by the lumbar pad.\n\nDesign goal #2 was design simplicity. This goal speaks to me because some of my favorite backpacks have high performance and function, but take a lot of time and effort to do things like use the load shelf or switch bags. Here is a huge win for KUIU, as this is one of the simplest designs in a hunting pack.\n\nTo use the load sling the hunter just needs to unbuckle the compression strap bucks, detach the bag attachment straps and lift the hypalon frame stay pockets off the upper frame stays. The top straps will squeeze through the frame stay pockets without removing the buckles from the straps. Switching pack bags is a breeze. The Icon 6 doesn’t have load stabilizer straps, so there is nothing to unthread when removing the pack, attaching the pack, or loading the sling.\n\nIn typical KUIU fashio"},{"text":"h daypack loads is awesome, but obviously it is not meant for hauling meat.\n\nThe previous generation pack-on-frame Icons were certainly capable of hauling just about anything I could strap to them. However, they just didn’t carry those loads as comfortably as some of the top performers. For me, two of those top performers are the Stone Glacier XCurve frame (with Sky 5900 pack) and the Kifaru ARK frame (with Bedlam pack). For this review, I made 1.2-mile loops (510 vertical feet gained and lost) with each pack loaded with 28 pounds of day hunting gear, followed by re-hiking the same loop with an additional 64 pounds of blasting media in a game bag to simulate an elk quarter. Each loop included portions on trail, off trail, and through deadfall.\n\nWith the packs loaded with day gear, it was difficult to really find the difference in comfort. I had zero belt slippage, no hot spots, and no shoulder discomfort with any of these three pack systems. The ARK frame was the stiffest, while the Icon 6 flexed a little more with the body, and the Xcurve was pretty laterally compliant.\n\nOf course, I fully expected bigger differences to emerge during the heavy load loops. I was pleasantly surprise"},{"text":"tigue with heavy loads. It didn’t matter how I tried to adjust the suspension. The Icon 6 completely remedied this for me, and brings the Icon 6 on the level with the very best frames available. Compared to the Xcurve, the Icon 6 had a much stiffer feel, but still flexed a bit when crawling over downed logs. The ARK is stiffer laterally, and … while still very comfortable … has more of a packboard-type feel. As a personal preference, I preferred the feel of the Icon 6 and ARK to the Xcurve in the heavy load testing.\n\nThe Icon 6 comes in two frame sizes. The Regular frame is 24.5 inches and the Tall frame is 26.5 inches. There are specific men’s and women’s suspensions available for these frames. My test Regular frame just under 10 inches wide and lumbar pad and top stays.\n\nI weighed the Icon 6 3500 with all the accessories I would actually use including both hip belt pockets and the Pack Load Hauler. The real world empty weight was 5 lbs. 12.5 ounces. This is in line with the Stone Glacier Sky 5900 (with belt pocket, lid, and water bottle holder) at 5 lbs. 13 ounces. The Kifaru ARK/Bedlam tips the scales over a pound heavier at exactly 7 lbs with a medium belt pocket and water bott"},{"text":"le holder.\n\nThe Icon 6 3500 base system including accessories comes in at $599. This pricing puts it squarely in the premium pack market but lower than top competitors. The Stone Glacier Sky 5900 is $725 without any accessories. The Kifaru ARK with the Bedlam runs a base price of $850. The Icon 6 is a downright bargain in the premium pack area especially when accounting for the included accessories.\n\nKUIU has elevated their Icon backpack line with their new Icon 6 frame and bags. The expressed design goals of improved heavy load comfort and simplified use have been satisfied. They have managed to maintain the low weights and organization they are known for while bringing the load-carrying performance and comfort up to compete with the very best in the market. It can not be overstated just how welcome and convenient a simple and intuitive design is for changing bags and converting for load hauling.\n\nEverything isn’t perfect with the Icon 6. The all-new design of the Icon 6 leaves existing Icon/Icon LT users with an incompatible system. I don’t quite get the daisy chain straps, and the Pack Load Hauler is finicky to get on the straps. Even so, there is no denying the Icon 6 system is"},{"text":"Store\n\nKifaru Crater Review\n\nSeek Outside Lanner 5400 Backpack Review\n\nTT#38 Saving a Buck with Robby Denning and Travis Hobbs"}]}

Cooperative Program\n\nRequest for Eagle Scout Certificate\n\nFederal Ballistic AE App Update Puts Data on One Screen\n\nFederal Ballistic AE App Update Puts Data on One Screen\n\nFederal’s redesigned iOS ballistics calculator merges its heads-up display and DOPE chart into one workspace and gets shooters to saved rifle profiles faster.\n\nSSUSA Staff\n\nAnnounced Oct. 15, the updated version of Federal’s Ballistic AE app for iOS merges its heads-up display and DOPE chart into a single interactive workspace.\n\nOn this ballistics calculator, real-time trajectory data sits right beside the adjustment figures. Plus, wind direction, wind speed and range can be tweaked manually without leaving the screen. Elevation, environmental upda"},{"text":"tes and wind corrections live there too, which cuts down on the toggling that eats up time behind the rifle.\n\nFederal cleaned up the rest of the layout as well. Rifles, profiles and loads are better organized, and favorites-based menus put frequently used setups a tap or two away. Bluetooth connectivity pairs the app with wind meters and other supporting devices, while live weather integration and GPS-aware tools round out the field kit. For hunters who spend their season well past the last bar of cell service, it also works offline.\n\nUnder the refresh, the ballistic engine carries over. Ballistic AE calculates trajectory, windage and velocity for Federal, Remington, Fiocchi and Speer projectiles, and its G1 and G7 ballistic coefficient calculator accounts for aerodynamic jump and spin drift. Results can be printed as DOPE cards or exported as charts.\n\nEvery rifle gets its own profile. Users can store optics configurations along with twist rate, barrel length, sight height and zero distance, then pull from an extensive factory ammunition database or a library of proprietary bullets for custom handloads. Classic and modern cartridges are both well represented, and ballistic data for"},{"text":" Federal’s Peak Alloy cartridges, including 7 mm Backcountry and 6.5 mm Creedmoor +Peak, comes built in.\n\n“At Federal, we’ve always embraced technology to help shooters and hunters hit their mark,” said Jason Nash, Federal’s vice president of marketing. “This new and improved mobile app provides a better user experience, will improve the accuracy of their system and make them a better shooter.”\n\nThe updated Ballistic AE app is available from the Apple App Store and at federalpremium.com.\n\nRelated Stories\n\nLongshot App Update Cuts Lag and Brings Audio From the Target\n\nBallistic App Review: Can “Free” Also Be “Best?”\n\nLongshot Overhauls Its Target Camera Contests\n\nNRA Day\n\nNRA Marksmanship Qualification Program\n\nYouth Education Summit\n\nAdventure Camp\n\nYouth Hunter Education Challenge\n\nNational Junior Shooting Camps\n\nYouth Wildlife Art Contest\n\nHome Air Gun Program\n\nNRA Junior Membership\n\nNRA Family\n\nEddie Eagle GunSafe® Program\n\nNRA Gun Safety Rules\n\nCollegiate Shooting Programs\n\nNational Youth Shooting Sports Cooperative Program\n\nRequest for Eagle Scout Certificate\n\nNew: ZeroTech Trace X 5-25X 56 mm FFP RMG-H MRAD Illuminated Riflescope\n\nNew: ZeroTech Trace X 5-25X 56 mm FFP RMG-H MRAD Illuminated Riflescope\n\nZeroTech’s latest Trace has 5X to 25X magnification, with 34 MRAD of elevation and a zero stop for less than $900.\n\nSSUSA Staff\n\nZeroTech Optics USA’s new Trace X 5-25X 56 mm FFP RMG-H MRAD Illuminated riflescope, a name nearly as long as some stage briefs, puts an illuminated reticle in the first focal plane and pairs it with turrets that click in tenths of a milliradian. The company is pointing it at PRS and NRL Hunter competitors, along with long-range hunters who would rather hold than dial.\n\nFirst focal plane is"},{"text":" what makes the package work. As the magnification ring turns, the RMG-H grid grows and shrinks with the image, so a 1.5-mil wind hold at 8X is still 1.5 mils at 22X. There’s no rolling to a calibrated power before taking the shot and no mental conversion if you forget. ZeroTech calls the RMG-H a precision hunting reticle, with 0.1- and 0.2-MRAD resolution for fine wind holds. Turned down low, heavy outer bars and an illuminated center give the eye somewhere to land on close, fast targets, which is exactly where many FFP reticles thin out to a hairline.\n\nThe scope offers 34 MRAD of total elevation travel and 17 MRAD of windage. A mechanical zero stop sits underneath it all, so after cranking up for the far plate, the elevation turret spins back down and stops at zero.\n\nLight comes in through a 56 mm objective with fully multi-coated lenses, and ZeroTech says some of the elements are German-made Schott glass. Field of view runs from 24.8 feet at 5X to 5 feet at 25X, both measured at 100 yards. Side-focus parallax adjusts from 22 yards to infinity, close enough to cover a rimfire training session at 25 yards. Eye relief is 3.82 inches, and the diopter adjusts from -3 to +2 with a loc"},{"text":"kring to keep it where you set it.\n\nEverything sits in a 34 mm maintube machined from 6061-T6 aluminum with an IPX7 rating. All in, the scope weighs 31.5 ounces.\n\nZeroTech ships it with a neoprene scope cover, rubber lens cover, hex keys, microfiber cloth and a printed reticle manual, plus a card with a QR code linking to the full instruction manual. Coverage falls under the company’s Triple-A Lifetime Warranty, which is fully transferable and requires no receipt.\n\nList price is $899, though ZeroTech’s website showed the Trace X 5-25X 56 mm at $749 at press time.\n\nLearn more at zerotechoptics.com.\n\nRelated Stories\n\nWhat’s In Your Range Bag, Colton Bagnoli?\n\nNew: Leupold Adds MOA Models To Mark 5HD Riflescope Family\n\nVideo: Contests\n\nNRA Day\n\nNRA Marksmanship Qualification Program\n\nYouth Education Summit\n\nAdventure Camp\n\nYouth Hunter Education Challenge\n\nNational Junior Shooting Camps\n\nYouth Wildlife Art Cooperative Program\n\nRequest for Eagle Scout Certificate\n\nNew: Trijicon REAP-IR-50 Thermal Riflescope Series\n\nNew: Trijicon REAP-IR-50 Thermal Riflescope Series\n\nTrijicon built the sensor core and software in-house, then packed laser ranging, ballistics, recording and live phone streaming into one rifle-mounted thermal optic.\n\nSSUSA Staff\n\nTrijicon’s new REAP-IR-50 series puts the ranging and the camera inside a thermal scope. Anyone who has carried a handheld rangefinder in a chest pocket, or clamped a phone behind the eyepiece to get the shot on video, can see the appeal.\n\nIt comes in two versions. The REAP-IR-50LRF adds an integrated laser rangefinder and ballistic solver, while the standard REAP-IR-50 estimates d"},{"text":"istance in stadiametric ranging mode using on-screen reference shapes. Both run on a sensor core and operating system developed in-house by Trijicon’s Electro Optics Division. Thus, the company decided which features make the cut, instead of designing around outside hardware.\n\nThe REAP-IR name has been in Trijicon’s thermal catalog for years, and the existing 35 mm and 60 mm models remain on the company’s website. Almost everything inside the REAP-IR-50 is new.\n\nAt the center sits a 1024x768 uncooled vanadium oxide sensor with a 12-micron pixel pitch, a solid jump in resolution over the 640-class sensors found in many high-end hunting thermals. It refreshes at 50 Hz, and Trijicon rates its thermal sensitivity at less than 25 millikelvin. The latter number describes how small a temperature difference the sensor can separate. Lower is better, and the advantage shows most on muggy or foggy nights when everything in the frame reads close to the same temperature.\n\nA manual-focus 50 mm f/1.0 objective gathers the scene, and a 1280x1024 color OLED display presents it. Base magnification is 1.5x, and digital zoom carries it to 12x. Because the reticle sits in the first focal plane, it scal"},{"text":"e display, and the scope works out an estimate. While slower and less precise than a laser, it trims a few ounces and, presumably, some cost.\n\nEight reticle patterns come in eight colors. Alongside standard, MOA and MRAD crosshairs and a circle dot, Trijicon includes bullet-drop compensating reticles matched to .223, .308, 300 BLK and 7.62x39 mm.\n\nControls got as much thought as the optics. A five-button keypad sits on the sloped rear face of the housing in an ambidextrous layout, with buttons big enough to find by feel in the dark or through gloves. Programmable Quick Keys put frequently used functions one press away, so there’s less menu diving with a target in view.\n\nThe REAP-IR-50 also doubles as a camera. Video and stills save to 64 GB of internal memory, and recoil-activated recording captures the shot automatically. Dedicated Wi-Fi links the scope to the Trijicon App on iOS and Android, where users can pull files to a phone or tablet or watch a live feed from the optic.\n\nLive View may be the most useful feature of the bunch. A guide can see exactly what the client sees, and a new hunter can learn to read a thermal picture without passing the rifle back and forth. Pitch and r"},{"text":"oll indicators, GPS, a compass and a microphone round out the electronics, while the USB-C port handles file transfers, software updates and external power.\n\nOn its two CR123 batteries alone, the REAP-IR-50 is rated for about 1.5 hours at room temperature, or roughly an hour while recording, and cold weather shortens that. Trijicon recommends an external pack and estimates 12 to 14 hours of runtime from a 10,000 mAh battery bank connected via USB-C. Anyone planning an all-night sit should count on carrying one and figuring out where to mount it.\n\nAs for durability, the aluminum housing wears a Cerakote FDE finish and was built and tested to MIL-STD-810H standards for shock, vibration and temperature extremes. It operates from −26° to 131°F, survives submersion to 1 meter for an hour and attaches with Trijicon’s Q-LOC Picatinny mount.\n\nThe REAP-IR-50LRF weighs 54.9 ounces, about 3.4 pounds, with its mount and lens cap but without batteries, while the standard model checks in at 51.4 ounces.\n\nAt $9,499 retail, the REAP-IR-50LRF ships with a hard case, the Q-LOC mount, a USB-C cable, two CR123 batteries and a LensPen. Price for the standard REAP-IR-50 is $8,999.\n\nTo learn more, visit "},{"text":"trijicon.com.\n\nRelated Stories\n\nNew: MDT One-Piece Night Vision Bridge\n\nNew: Trijicon SRO (Specialized Reflex Optic)\n\nNew: Swarovski Optik tM 35 Thermal Clip-On Device\n\nNew: Trijicon RMRcc Miniature Reflex Optic\n\nWATCH: Swarovski Optik Z5(i)+ Riflescopes\n\nMost Popular\n\nMore like this from around the NRA\n\nLatest\n\nFederal Ballistic AE App Update Puts Data on One Screen\n\nFederal’s updated Ballistic AE app puts its heads-up display and DOPE chart on one screen and pairs with Bluetooth wind meters like the Kestrel.\n\nNew for 2026: Hornady Reloading Products\n\nHornady’s reloading lineup now includes the Lock-N-Load Classic Pro Accuracy Kit, the Jet Blower, a Universal Mounting Plate and 6.8 Western components.\n\nNew: ZeroTech Trace X 5-25X 56 mm FFP RMG-H MRAD Illuminated Riflescope\n\nZeroTech’s Trace X 5-25X 56 mm pairs an illuminated FFP MRAD reticle with 0.1-MRAD turrets and a zero stop for long-range competitors.\n\nBeretta and La Vallée Build a Shotgun You Can See Into\n\nBeretta and watchmaker La Vallée have unveiled the SO10 Kronos, a one-off sidelock with sapphire-windowed sideplates built for Beretta’s 500th anniversary.\n\nChad Heckler Wins Limited Title at 2026 IPRF World Championship\n\n"}]}

Cantilever mounts and accessories work as one modular family, so a red dot, clip-on thermal or laser rangefinder can join a riflescope without improvised hardware.\n\nSSUSA Staff\n\nVortex has a fix for the rifle with a top rail that has turned into a pile of adapters. Its new Pro Cantilever Mounts and Accessories, made in the U.S., are designed as a single system. You start with the Pro Cantilever Mount, then bolt on offset or overhead red dots, clip-on thermals or a laser rangefinder as the rifle takes on new work.\n\nYou can get the"},{"text":" mount for 30 mm or 34 mm tubes, with a choice of 1.54- or 1.93-inch ring heights. Like any cantilever, it pushes the rings out ahead of the rail clamps so an AR-style rifle has room for proper eye relief. Vortex says the two heights cover everything from an ordinary riflescope to setups built around clip-ons and night-vision devices. Someone there also had the good sense to print the torque specifications right on the ring caps. Right now, it’s priced at $179.99 on Vortex’s website.\n\nIf you run a low-power variable optic and want a red dot off to the side for close targets, the Pro Cantilever 45-Degree Offset Red Dot Mount attaches to the cantilever base. Roll the rifle a few degrees and the dot is waiting. Anyone who shoots Three-Gun has made that move a thousand times. It takes optics with a DeltaPoint Pro footprint and sells for $89.99.\n\nNot everyone likes tipping the rifle mid-stage. For them, there’s the Pro 12 O’Clock Red Dot Mount at $104.99, which sets a DeltaPoint Pro-footprint dot directly over the main optic. Vortex makes it in both tube sizes, and it fits the new Pro Riflescope Rings as well as the cantilever mounts.\n\nThen there’s the Pro Diving Board Mount, a forward "},{"text":"shelf for gear with nowhere else to go. That might be a red dot, an infrared illuminator or a rangefinder like Vortex’s own Impact 4000 Ballistic Rail-Mounted Laser Rangefinder. Sizing and compatibility match the 12 o’clock mount. It runs $119.99.\n\nYou can order the mounts and accessories from vortexoptics.com, or use the company’s store locator to find a local dealer.\n\nRelated Stories\n\nNew: MDT 35 mm High One-Piece Scope Mount\n\nReview: Leapers UTG Pro P.O.I. None of the camping blankets we tested carry standardized temperature ratings like sleeping bags do, so comparisons between brands rely on fill weight and real-world testing. As a general guide, lighter blankets under 20 oz work well for campfire sitting and mild summer nights, while heavier insulated options like the Rumpl Backcountry Blanket can handle cold ridgelines and frosty desert mornings.\n\nIs down or synthetic insulation better for a camping blanket?\n\nDown offers the best warmth-to-weight ratio and compresses smaller than synthetic fill, but it loses insulating power when wet and is far more difficult to wash, often requiring specialty detergent and hours of drying time. Synthetic insulation stays warm even when damp, machine washes easily, and costs less, but is bulkier for the same warmth. For blankets that will see regular outdoor use and washing, synthetic is the more practical choice. For travel or home use where packability matters most and washing is infrequent, down is worth the tradeoff.\n\nAre camping blankets waterproof?\n\nWaterproofnes"},{"text":"test Known Time (FKT) record holder on the Appalachian Trail, Liz came to Treeline Review from New York Times/Wirecutter, the New York Times’ product review site, where she was a staff writer on the outdoor team.\n\nLiz has talked gear on Good Morning America (TV), in The Wall Street Journal, The Guardian, Buzzfeed, Men’s Journal, Women’s Health, Gizmodo, and Outside Magazine.\n\nShe first encountered puffy blankets at the Outdoor Retailer trade show in 2016.
